0\\nOriginal token count: 1614\\nOutput:\\n# Extended documentation for data stored in multi_anim_sess\\n\\nAs an example, let's take: multi_anim_sess file 2-3-4-6-7-10-11-12-13-14-15-17-18-19_expday3_speed2_perms100_maximin_events.pickle.\\n\\nThis filename means:\\n\\n* it includes mice m2, m3, m4, m6, m7, m10, m11, m12, m12, m13, m14, m15, m17, m18, m19. 2,3,4,7,11,12,13,14,15,17,18,19 learned the reward zone switch task.\\n* it contains data from experiment day 3 (the 1st switch day)\\n* place cells were identified taking neural activity at running speeds >2 cm/s\\n* 100 permutations were used for place cell significance calculations\\n* the dF/F baseline was calculated using a `maximin` filter\\n* deconvolved calcium `events` were used to define place cells\\n\\nEach pickle file is a collection of dictionaries, with a key for each animal, plus a key for the `params` used to define place cells. Animals were originally named by indicator (i.e. GCAMP) and a number.\\n\\nIf you call the entry for a given animal, say \\\"GCAMP4\\\", it is a dictionary with keys: \\n```\\ndict_keys(['animal', 'sess', 'pc masks set0', 'pc masks set1', 'SI set0', 'SI set1', 'p set0', 'p set1', 'pc perms set0', 'pc perms set1', 'SI perms set0', 'SI perms set1', 'isreward', 'morph', 'dream', 'rzone', 'rz label', 'trial dict'])\\n```\\n\\n* `pc masks setx` is the boolean indicator telling you which unit/cell is a place cell or not in each trial set, where True means p<0.05 that it's a place cell\\n    * set0 is the 30 trials before the reward switch, set1 is all the trials after the reward switch\\n    * if there was no reward switch that day, set0 is the first half, set1 is the second half\\n    * I typically define \\\"overall\\\" place cells to be cells with significant spatial information (p<0.05, True) in set0 OR set1\\n* `sess` is a class created by the TwoPUtils repo with the neural and behavioral data from the session\\n* `SI`, `p` for each trial set are the spatial information per cell and p-value compared to shuffle per cell. The `perms` or shuffle information were not saved on figshare to reduce filesize.\\n* `is_reward` refers to a 1 x N trials binary array of whether a given trial was rewarded or not (1=rewarded, 0=omission trial)\\n* `morph` is a 1 x N trials binary array of which environment was active (0=Env 1, 1=Env 2)\\n* `dream` is something unused from another experiment, ignore it (it will be all zeros)\\n* `rzone` is a 2 x N trials array of the reward zone start and end coordinates, in cm\\n* `rz label` is a 1 x N trials array with text labels for which reward zone was active ('A', 'B', 'C')\\n* `trial dict` is a dictionary with entries `trial_set0` and `trial_set1`, which are each boolean arrays indicating which trials were included in which trial set.\\n\\nWithin multi_anim_sess['GCAMP4']['sess'], there are attributes: \\n```\\ndict_keys(['vr_data', 'trial_start_inds', 'teleport_inds', 'scan_info', 'n_planes', 'timeseries', 'trial_matrices', 'iscell', 'plane_per_cell', 's2p_ops', 's2p_stats', 'basedir_2P', 'basedir_VR', 'mouse', 'date', 'scene', 'session', 'scan_number', 'scanner', 'VR_only', 'scan_file', 'scanheader_file', 'vr_filename', 's2p_path', 'prompt_for_keys', 'verbose', 'basedir'])\\n```\\n\\n* `timeseries` is a dict containing the variables: dict_keys(['F', 'Fneu', 'licks', 'rewards', 'speed', 'events', 'dff'])\\n    *  each 'timeseries' entry is a cells x samples array, where each sample is a single imaging frame sampled at ~15.5 Hz (each frame is ~64.5 ms)\\n    * 'F' is the raw fluorescence of each cell ROI\\n    * 'Fneu' is the fluorescence in a small \\\"donut\\\" around the ROI that is subtracted in preprocessing before you get \\\"dff\\\"\\n    * 'dff' is delta F/F\\n    * 'events' is the deconvolved spike-like activity from 'dff' (but it's not really a spike rate, it just gets rid of the asymmetric smoothing of the calcium kernel)\\n    * 'licks' are a cumulative lick count in each frame, so in my behavior code anything >1 gets set to 1 to be able to convert it accurately to a rate when I want to convert it to Hz per position bin or time bin\\n    * 'rewards' is just a binary at the sample where reward was delivered\\n    * 'speed' is an actual smoothed running speed in cm/s for each sample.\\n* `trial_matrices` are the spatially binned versions of timeseries, in bins of 10 cm, from 0 to 450 cm (not including the teleport zone). Shape: trials x position bins x cells.\\n* `trial_start_inds` is a 1 x N trials array of the sample index for the start of each trial (i.e. the start of the track)\\n* `teleport_indices` is a 1 x N trials array of the sample index for the start of the teleport period (i.e. the end of the track)\\n* `vr_data` is a Pandas dataframe of VR behavior data converted from the sqlite files output by Unity and already synchronized with the imaging data\\n\\nThe sess class, which aligns the VR data to the imaging data, is generated by [preprocessing.vr_align_to_2P](https://github.com/GiocomoLab/TwoPUtils/blob/00b6589e5afea4cfe2347557b0a2450d9673c435/TwoPUtils/preprocessing.py#L124) in the [TwoPUtils](https://github.com/GiocomoLab/TwoPUtils/tree/main) repo. Please review this function carefully to understand how the VR data are pre-processed and written into sess.vr_data.\\n\\nIn multi_anim_sess['GCAMP4']['sess'].vr_data, there is one row per sample (imaging frame), and the relevant columns are:\\n* `time`: the timestamp of the sample, in the imaging frame rate (VR data have already been synchronized with imaging)\\n* `pos`: Animal's position in cm on a virtual linear track\\n* `trialnum`: trial number, where each trial is a lap of the track\\n* `dz`: delta in position from one sample to the next\\n* `speed`: the speed of the mouse in cm/s\\n* `lick`: lick detection by capacitive sensor; preprocessed cumulatively per imaging frame\\n* `reward`: a binary of when reward was delivered\\n* `rzone`: a binary flag of when the animal entered the reward zone; preprocessed cumulatively per imaging frame\\n* `morph`: the identity of the virtual environment (see above)\\n* `autoreward`: whether trial was automatically rewarded if the mouse failed to lick\\n* `tstart`: start of a trial, i.e. entry to the linear track\\n* `teleport`: end of a trial, i.e. entry to the linear track\\n* `scanning`: whether scanning occurred to collect imaging data (1 if scanning)\\n\\nAll other vr_data columns are unused or can safely be ignored.\\n\\nIn samples prior to when Unity started sending TTLs to the imaging computer to synchronize acquisition, `morph`, `trialnum`, and `scanning` have values set to -1, and `pos` is set to -500. \\n\"\n   694→          }\n   695→        ]\n   696→      },\n   697→      \"extra\": {\n   698→ VR provided tight control over sensory stimuli and the animal\\u2019s trajectory, allowing us to observe neuronal remapping in relation to distant rewards, a phenomenon potentially less visible in freely moving scenarios. Furthermore, the task included multiple updates to a hidden reward zone across two environments, allowing us to dissociate spatially driven and reward-driven remapping. Imaging began on day\\u20091 of task acquisition, in which animals traversed a unidirectional 450\\u2009cm virtual linear track (for example, Environment 1 (ENV 1)) with a hidden 50\\u2009cm reward zone where sucrose water was delivered operantly for licking (Fig. 1d,e). On day\\u20093, the reward zone was moved within a \\u2018switch\\u2019 session to a new track location, signaled by automatic reward delivery on the first ten post-switch trials only if the mouse failed to lick in the new zone. On day\\u20095, the reward zone was moved to a third location, then returned to its original location on day\\u20097. On day\\u20098, the reward switch coincided with the introduction of a novel environment (for example, ENV 2), in which the switch order was then reversed (Fig. 1e). On \\u2018stay\\u2019 days, the last reward location from the previous day was maintained. A separate \\u2018fixed-condition\\u2019 group of mice (n\\u2009=\\u20093) experienced only one reward location in a single environment (ENV 1) (Extended Data Fig. 1), to control for the effect of experience. To maintain engagement, the reward was randomly omitted on ~15% of trials. At the end of the track, mice passed through a variable length gray \\u2018teleport\\u2019 zone (~50\\u2009cm\\u2009+\\u2009temporal jitter up to 10\\u2009s; Methods) before starting the next lap.\\n13:The main \\u2018switch\\u2019 task involved two virtual environments similar to those previously used to study hippocampal remapping, each with visually distinct features from the pre-training environment. Both environments consisted of a 450\\u2009cm linear track, with two colored towers (green in ENV 1, blue in ENV 2) and two patterned towers along the walls. Environments were further distinguished by the frequency of diagonal wall gratings (low, ENV 1; high, ENV 2) and color of the sky (dark gray, ENV 1; light gray, ENV 2). The reward zone was a \\u2018hidden\\u2019, unmarked 50\\u2009cm span at one of three possible locations along the track, each equidistantly spaced between the towers to control for proximity of each reward to visual landmarks: zone A, 80\\u2013130\\u2009cm; zone B, 200\\u2013250\\u2009cm; zone C, 320\\u2013370\\u2009cm. Only one reward zone was ever active at a time. Reward was randomly omitted on approximately 15% of trials, determined by a random number generator for each trial. Each lap terminated in a black curtain following by a \\u2018teleport period\\u2019 that began with an unchanging gray display for a randomly jittered amount of time (\\u2018jitter period\\u2019) between 1 and 5\\u2009s (5\\u201310\\u2009s on trials following reward omissions or trials in which the mouse did not lick in the reward zone), after which the gray display began to move again for 50\\u2009cm giving the appearance of a gray \\u2018tunnel,\\u2019 before the mouse re-entered the virtual environment at 0\\u2009cm.\\n15:Each mouse encountered a different starting reward zone and sequence of reward zone switches, counterbalanced across mice (n\\u2009=\\u200911 mice). Most mice began the task in ENV 1 (n\\u2009=\\u20099 mice; two mice (m17 and m18) began in ENV 2). An initial reward zone (for example, A as in Fig. 1e) was acquired for days\\u20091\\u20132 of the task. On day\\u20093 (switch one), the zone was moved after 30 trials to one of the two other possible locations on the track (for example, B). On the first ten trials of any new condition, including the first day and each subsequent switch, the reward was automatically delivered at the end of the zone if the mouse had not yet licked within the zone, to signal reward availability. Otherwise, the reward was delivered at the first location within the zone where the mouse licked. After these ten trials, a reward was operantly delivered for licking within the zone, and we observed that the mice often started licking before ten trials elapsed (Extended Data Fig. 1a\\u2013c). The new reward zone was maintained on day\\u20094. On day\\u20095 (switch two), the zone was moved to the third possible reward zone (for example, C), maintained on day\\u20096 and moved back to the original location on day\\u20097 (for example, A). On day\\u20098, the reward zone switch coincided with a switch into the novel environment, where the sequence of zone switches was then reversed on the same day-to-day schedule for a total of 14\\u2009days (Fig. 1 and Extended Data Fig. 1). Each switch occurred after 30 trials.\\n19:We targeted 80\\u2013100 trials per session with simultaneous 2P imaging (described below), with one imaging session per day (mean\\u2009\\u00b1\\u2009s.d., 80.5\\u2009\\u00b1\\u20097.4 trials across 14 mice, all imaging days). The session was terminated early if the mouse ceased licking or running consistently and/or if the imaging time exceeded 50\\u2009min. Before the imaging session, mice were provided 30 \\u2018warm-up\\u2019 trials using the task and reward zone from the previous day to re-acclimate them to the VR setup (using the pre-training environment on day\\u20091 of imaging). Following each daily imaging session, mice were given another ~100 training trials without imaging on the last reward zone seen in the imaging session until they acquired their daily water allotment.\\n23:We used a resonant-galvo scanning 2P microscope from Neurolabware with Scanbox software (https://scanbox.org, v.4.7) operated through MATLAB (Mathworks) to image the calcium activity of CA1 neurons. Excitation was achieved using 920\\u2009nm light from a tunable output femtosecond pulsed laser (Coherent Chameleon Discovery). Laser power modulation was accomplished with a Pockels cell (Conoptics). The typical excitation power, measured at the front face of the objective (Leica \\u00d725, 1.0\\u2009NA, 2.6\\u2009mm working distance), ranged from 15\\u201368\\u2009mW for mice m2\\u2013m7, 50\\u2013100\\u2009mW for m10 and m11, and 13\\u201340\\u2009mW for m12\\u201319. The task was imaged starting from day\\u20091 for all mice except m11, for whom imaging started on day\\u20093 owing to lower viral expression. For most animals and sessions, to minimize photodamage and photobleaching, the Pockels cell was used to reduce laser power to minimal levels between trials (during the teleport period), except for mice m11\\u2013m14 on task days 1, 7, 8 and 14, and mice m15\\u2013m19 on day\\u20091 and all switch days, for which laser power was maintained and imaging continued throughout the teleport period. Photons were collected using Hamamatsu gated GAsP photomultiplier tubes (part H11706-401 for Neurolabware Microscopes). The imaging field of view (FOV) was collected at 1.0 magnification with unidirectional scanning at ~15.5\\u2009Hz, resulting in an approximately 0.7\\u2009\\u00d7\\u20090.7\\u2009mm FOV (512\\u2009\\u00d7\\u2009796 pixels). An electrically tunable lens was used to simultaneously image deep and superficial CA1 in two mice (m17 and m18), in two planes separated by ~27\\u2009\\u00b5m, with frames bidirectionally imaged at ~31\\u2009Hz interleaved in the scan for a sampling rate of ~15.5\\u2009Hz per plane. In all mice, the same FOV was acquired each session by aligning to a reference image from previous days before the start of data acquisition, with the aid of the \\u2018searchref\\u2019 plugin in Scanbox. This allowed us to track single cells across days.\\n27:The Suite2P software package (v.0.10.3) was used to perform xy motion correction (rigid and non-rigid) and identify putative cell regions of interest (ROIs). Manual curation eliminated ROIs containing multiple somata or dendrites, lacking visually obvious transients, suspected of overexpressing the calcium indicator or exhibiting high and continuous fluorescence fluctuation typical of putative interneurons. This approach yielded 155\\u20132172 putative pyramidal neurons per session, owing to variation in imaging window implant quality and viral expression. In multi-plane imaged animals (see \\u20182P imaging\\u2019), ROIs were identified separately per plane, but planes were pooled for all analyses except those in Extended Data Fig. 7. Custom code was used to follow individual ROIs across imaging sessions using the intersection over union of their pixels. The threshold for ROI matching was chosen algorithmically for each dataset such that the intersection over union for the best match for an ROI pair was always greater than the intersection over union for any second-best match.\\n29:To compute the \\u0394F/F (dF/F) for each ROI, baseline fluorescence was calculated within each trial independently using a maximin procedure with a 20\\u2009s sliding window. Limitation to individual trials both accounts for potential photobleaching over the session and avoids the teleport periods for sessions during which the laser power was reduced (see \\u20182P imaging\\u2019). The value of dF/F was then calculated for each cell as the fluorescence minus the baseline, divided by the absolute value of the baseline, then smoothed with a two-sample (~0.129\\u2009s) s.d. Gaussian kernel. The activity rate was extracted by deconvolving dF/F with a canonical calcium kernel using the OASIS algorithm as used in Suite2p. We note that this deconvolution is not interpreted as a spike rate but rather as a method to eliminate the asymmetric smoothing of the calcium signal induced by indicator kinetics. Additional putative interneurons were detected for exclusion from further analysis by a Pearson correlation of >0.5 between their dF/F timeseries and the animal\\u2019s running speed, excluding 0.42\\u2009\\u00b1\\u20090.85% of cells (mean\\u2009\\u00b1\\u2009s.d. across mice and days).\\n37:The number of mice to include was determined by having coverage of every possible reward sequence permutation (six sequences) with at least one mouse in the switch task. In addition, our mouse sample sizes were similar to those reported in previous publications. Mice were randomly selected to experience the switch task (n\\u2009=\\u200911 mice) versus the \\u2018fixed-condition\\u2019 task in which the reward zone was not moved (n\\u2009=\\u20093).\\n41:AP and ML ROI coordinates were computed as the 2D center of mass of ROI pixels, relative to each FOV. In multi-plane imaged animals, ROIs were separated according to their deep or superficial imaging plane. To estimate deep-superficial (dorso-ventral (DV)) coordinates in single-plane animals, we acquired a z-stack scan after the day\\u200914 task session that extended \\u2212100\\u2009\\u00b5m below (\\u221260\\u2009\\u00b5m below in one mouse) to +100\\u2009\\u00b5m above the FOV in the DV axis, in 2\\u2009\\u00b5m steps with ~50 frames per step. Each step was independently motion-corrected as described above, and the mean of each step was used to compile the 3D z-stack array. We then took the AP projection of each ML slice of the z-stack (that is, looking at the side view of the z-stack). We minimum-filtered (4\\u2009\\u00d7\\u20090 pixel window, ML\\u2009\\u00d7\\u2009DV) and smoothed (2\\u2009\\u00d7\\u20092 pixel s.d. Gaussian) each slice, then detected the most prominent dip in brightness in the DV axis to approximate the location of cell bodies, under the assumption that the GCaMP signal is typically absent in the nuclei and brighter in the surrounding neuropil. This yielded a per-pixel estimate of the depth of the pyramidal layer center from the dorsal surface of the z-stack, which we then smoothed (40\\u2009\\u00d7\\u200940 pixel s.d. Gaussian) to approximate the cell layer \\u2018curvature\\u2019 across the FOV. The curvature estimate was registered to the mean FOV of each imaging session. Given that the imaging plane transects this curvature, calculating the mean DV distance of each ROI (across pixels) from the curvature provides an estimate of where each ROI resides in the deep-to-superficial axis.\\n45:The capacitive lick sensor allowed us to detect single licks. A very small number of trials with erroneous lick detection from damage to the circuit were removed from subsequent licking analysis by setting these values to NaN (~0.65% of all imaged trials, n\\u2009=\\u200981 out of 12,376 trials removed across 11 switch mice). These trials were detected by >30% of the 0.0645\\u2009s imaging frame samples in the trial containing a cumulative lick count >2, as this would have produced a sustained lick rate \\u226520\\u2009Hz. Remaining lick counts were converted to a binary vector and spatially binned at the same resolution as neural activity (10\\u2009cm bins), then divided by the time occupancy in each bin to yield a lick rate. We quantified licking precision over blocks of ten trials using an anticipatory lick ratio, computed as:\\n55:We defined place cells as cells with significant SI65 in either trial set 1 (pre-reward-switch) or trial set 2 (post-reward-switch) in a session. On days without a switch, we used the first and second halves of trials. For the SI, we used the deconvolved calcium activity as this reduces the asymmetry of the calcium signal. SI was calculated for calcium imaging as:\\n59:where pi is the occupancy probability in position bin i for the whole session, fi is the trial-averaged activity per position bin i and f is the mean activity over the whole session, computed as the sum of fi\\u2009\\u00d7\\u2009pi over all N bins. To determine pi, we calculated the per-trial occupancy (number of imaging samples) in each bin divided by the total number of samples per trial, then summed the occupancy probabilities across trials and divided by the total per session. To determine the significance of the SI scores, we created a null distribution by circularly permuting the position data relative to the timeseries of each cell, by a random amount between ~1\\u2009s and the length of the trial, independently on each trial. SI was calculated from the trial-averaged activity of each shuffle, and this shuffle procedure was repeated 100 times per cell. A cell\\u2019s true SI was considered significant if it exceeded 95% of the SI scores from all shuffles within an animal, pooled across cells (more stringent than comparing to the shuffle of each individual cell). For plotting place cell firing over trials (for example, Fig. 2), deconvolved calcium activity was normalized to the mean of each cell within a session and smoothed with a 10\\u2009cm\\u2009s.d. Gaussian.\\n67:Correlation matrices were computed using the spatially binned deconvolved activity on each trial, smoothed with a 20\\u2009cm\\u2009s.d. Gaussian for single cells and a 10\\u2009cm\\u2009s.d. Gaussian for population vectors. This resulted in a matrix, A, of j trials\\u2009\\u00d7\\u2009m position bins per cell. For population vectors, single-cell matrices were horizontally concatenated such that A was j trials\\u2009\\u00d7\\u2009m position bins\\u2009\\u00d7\\u2009n cells. Each trial was z-scored across the position axis. For single cells, the trial-by-trial correlation matrix C was computed as:\\n73:We defined the cell remapping types shown in Fig. 2 and Extended Data Fig. 2 as follows: TR, significant SI before and after the switch, with spatial peaks (see \\u2018Spatial peak firing identification\\u2019) \\u226450\\u2009cm from each other before versus after; disappearing, significant SI before but not after the switch, with a mean spatially binned dF/F after that is less than the 50th percentile of the per-trial mean dF/F before; appearing, significant SI after but not before, with a mean spatially binned dF/F after that is greater than 1\\u2009s.d. above the mean in trials before (appearing and disappearing cells that had sufficiently reliable activity despite their rate-remapping to be captured by the RR criteria below were removed from the appearing and disappearing groups); remap near reward, significant SI before and after, with spatial peaks \\u226450\\u2009cm from the starts of both reward zones; and remap far from reward, significant SI before and after, not TR, with peaks >50\\u2009cm from the start of at least one reward zone.\\n79:For stringent statistics on putative RR remapping (Fig. 2c\\u2013i and Extended Data Fig. 2g\\u2013n), we restricted the set of included place cells to require significant SI in the pre-switch and post-switch trial sets in each session. We converted the position timeseries of the animal to periodic coordinates, setting 0\\u2009cm on the track to \\u2212\\u03c0 and 450\\u2009cm to \\u03c0. Spatial peaks for each cell were re-computed using binned dF/F at a periodic bin size of 2\\u03c0\\u2009/\\u200945 (corresponding to ~10\\u2009cm). Putative TR cells were removed by excluding cells with spatial peaks within ~0.698 radians (50\\u2009cm) of each other before versus after the switch. The periodic coordinates were then circularly rotated to align the start of each reward zone at 0, to measure the signed circular distance of spatial peaks relative to the start of each reward zone. For scatter plots of spatial peaks in Fig. 2 and Extended Data Fig. 2, points are jittered by a random amount between \\u2212\\u03c0\\u2009/\\u2009100 and +\\u03c0\\u2009/\\u2009100 for visualization only. We measured the circularly wrapped difference between relative peaks after minus before the switch, creating a distribution that can be thought of as orthogonal to the unity line in Fig. 2d,f. We compared this distribution to a \\u2018random-remapping\\u2019 shuffle, generated by maintaining the pre-switch peak of each cell and circularly permuting its post-switch firing 1,000 times by a random offset of 0\\u201344 bins. To define a candidate range of RR remapping variability, we computed the circular difference between the maximum and minimum bin of the distribution of true differences between RR peaks that exceeded the upper 95% of the shuffle, divided by two and averaged across switches. In an initial cohort of n\\u2009=\\u20097 mice, this produced a mean range of \\u00b10.656 radians (~46.9\\u2009cm) around 0, which is captured by a 50\\u2009cm threshold given our 10\\u2009cm bin size. This threshold was subsequently used to identify candidate RR remapping cells (see below).\\n83:As we observed variable remapping dynamics over trials after a reward switch (Fig. 2a,b and Extended Data Fig. 2a), which could affect SI scores, we relaxed the SI criterion for RR cells such that they were required to have significant SI either before or after the switch but not necessarily both. We next implemented two criteria to identify a robust subpopulation of RR cells. First, we identified place cells with peak spatial firing relative to the reward zone start within ~0.698 radians (50\\u2009cm) of each other before versus after a reward switch (within the orange candidate zone highlighted in Fig. 2d,f), allowing for some variability in RR precision. These cells could thus include \\u2018remap near reward\\u2019 and \\u2018remap far from reward\\u2019 cells (see \\u2018Remapping category definitions\\u2019). Second, to reduce the influence of noise in spatial peak detection and account for the shape of the firing fields, we cross-correlated the cells\\u2019 trial-averaged, spatially binned dF/F aligned to the reward zone pre-switch versus post-switch. We predicted that RR cells should have a peak cross-correlation close to zero on this reward-centered axis. We calculated a shuffle for the cross-correlation by circularly permuting the activity in each post-switch trial by a random offset between 1 and 45 bins 500 times, requiring that the real cross-correlogram had a peak exceeding the upper 97.5% confidence interval of the shuffle and that the offset of this peak was within five bins (~50\\u2009cm) of zero. This approach provides a metric for how stable each cell\\u2019s firing is relative to reward, identifying a subpopulation of cells whose fields were maximally Pearson-correlated in periodic coordinates relative to reward, in contrast to the TR cells, which were maximally correlated relative to the original linear track coordinates (Extended Data Fig. 2o\\u2013q). Place cells that passed both criteria were defined as RR.\\n93:Using the spatially binned, deconvolved activity smoothed with a 10\\u2009cm\\u2009s.d. Gaussian, excluding activity from movement speeds <2\\u2009cm\\u2009s\\u22121, candidate place fields were identified as regions of \\u226520\\u2009cm that exceeded 20% of the maximum of the trial-averaged activity19 (over the 30 pre-switch trials or the final 30 post-switch trials). To include a given field, the cell was required to be significantly active within the field boundaries in at least eight (>25%) of the 30 trials, where \\u2018significantly active\\u2019 was defined as the raw, un-binned deconvolved activity at those positions exceeding its mean +1\\u2009s.d. across the 30 trials. For analyses of place field properties before versus after a reward switch, cells were excluded that did not have significant SI or a significant field both before and after the switch. The fraction of cell remapping types meeting these criteria on specific days is reported in Extended Data Fig. 3c. For analyses of place field width and in-field activity rate, cells were further excluded if one of their fields overlapped the first or last spatial bin of the track, as this prevented accurate detection of field boundaries that extended into the teleport zone (which was not imaged for some animals; see \\u20182P imaging\\u2019). Place field width was defined as the last minus the first position bin that crossed the initial 20% activity threshold.\\n97:We assessed the alignment of the center of mass of each field to RR or TR coordinates. We first confirmed that for cells with one field before and after the switch, the field center of mass replicated the robust RR and TR remapping patterns (Extended Data Fig. 3e) observed for spatial firing peaks (Fig. 2c,d,f). Next, for the subset of cells that had exactly two fields both before and after the switch, we computed the circular\\u2013circular correlation of the offsets between fields before versus after the switch (Extended Data Fig. 3f,g).\\n100:To quantify the degree of backward and forward shifting of place fields following a reward zone switch, we first identified the formation lap (trial) of each field post-switch by adapting a previously established procedure31. Within the bounds of each trial-averaged field \\u00b110\\u2009cm after the switch, we identified trials in which the post-switch spatially binned activity exceeded its mean +1\\u2009s.d. We then found the first five-trial window with at least three trials above this threshold and took the first active trial in that window as the formation lap31. To compute the field\\u2019s center of mass on each lap with maximum spatial resolution, we used the raw deconvolved activity and position within the bounds of the trial-averaged field. We then subtracted the center of mass on the formation lap from the center of mass of the field\\u2019s mean activity in the last 30 trials of the session to quantify the degree of shift (negative values indicate a backwards shift).\\n102:# Decoding of RR position\\n104:We implemented a previously developed decoder that uses circular\\u2013linear regression38 to predict the animal\\u2019s position in circular, RR coordinates from the deconvolved calcium event timeseries (at speeds of >2\\u2009cm\\u2009s\\u22121) of RR, TR or non-RR remapping neurons. The model \\u2018decode score\\u2019 at each timepoint t is given by \\\\cos ({{y}_{t}}-\\\\hat{{y}}_{t}), where is the animal\\u2019s true RR position and \\\\hat{{y}}_{t} is the predicted RR position. A score of one indicates perfect prediction and zero indicates random prediction. We performed tenfold cross-validation in which we trained the decoder on 90% of the data and tested it on a held-out 10%, either training and testing within the trials before the switch or training on one set (before) and testing on the other set (after). Figure 3a shows decoder performance from a single cross-validation fold, considering all data from an example session. To quantify decoding accuracy compared to chance, we next downsampled the data to match occupancy at each RR position bin (bin size 2\\u03c0\\u2009/\\u200945, ~10\\u2009cm), either within trials before the switch or in the last 30 trials after the switch (thereby matching trial numbers before and after). We then repeated model training and testing with 100 shuffled datasets for each session and cell subpopulation. In each shuffle, the timeseries of each cell was independently circularly shifted by a uniform random amount up to the number of samples in the session minus one. In Fig. 3b, model performance is reported as a mean decode score over time samples and cross-validation folds compared to the mean of the shuffles for each session. To evaluate decoder performance across sessions, we z-scored the decoder score of each session to its shuffle and binned the z-score as a function of RR position, then averaged across animals (Fig. 3c).\\n108:For analysis of behavioral timescale sequential firing of neural subpopulations, we used the unsmoothed, spatially binned dF/F in circular track coordinates (not aligned to reward, although aligning to reward makes no difference for the circular sequence order). We sorted neurons by their peak firing positions from activity averaged over the odd trials before the switch. This sort was cross-validated by plotting the trial-averaged activity of the even trials before the switch (for example, left-hand columns of Fig. 4a, normalized to the mean activity of each cell within a session), then applying this sort to the trials after the switch. The sequence positions before versus after were then taken as the peak firing positions of the trial-averaged \\u2018even-before\\u2019 trials and the trial-averaged \\u2018after\\u2019 trials. Preservation of sequence order was quantified as the circular\\u2013circular correlation coefficient between the sequence positions before versus after. Although a P\\u2009value can be directly computed for this correlation coefficient, its significance was further validated by randomly permuting the cell identities of the neurons after the switch and re-computing the correlation coefficient 1,000 times to obtain a null distribution. The observed correlation coefficient, rho, was considered significant if it was outside 95% of the null distribution using a two-tailed test. The P value was calculated as:\\n112:where {n}_{\\\\left|{{\\\\rm{coef}}_{\\\\rm{shuf}}}\\\\right|\\\\ge \\\\left|{{\\\\rm{coef}}_{\\\\rm{obs}}}\\\\right|} is the number of shuffled coefficients with absolute values greater than or equal to the absolute value of the observed coefficient, and {N}_{\\\\rm{perm}} is the total number of shuffles. P\\u2009<\\u20090.001 indicates that the observed rho exceeded all 1,000 shuffles.\\n124:In the sessions for which we imaged the whole teleport period (see \\u20182P imaging\\u2019), we re-identified place cells including activity during the \\u2018tunnel\\u2019 and part of the \\u2018jitter\\u2019 in the teleport period (see \\u2018Behavioral training and VR tasks\\u2019 for description), and re-categorized remapping types as either TR or RR from this set of place cells. Given that the length of each jitter was different, we only included spatial bins during the jitter that were occupied with fewer than ten trials missing in the session, using spatial bins of 10\\u2009cm starting at \\u221250 cm (the start of the tunnel) up to the maximum occupied bin (range, 530\\u2013580\\u2009cm from the start of the virtual track at 0\\u2009cm). We then re-performed the population sequence analysis (see \\u2018Sequence detection and quantification\\u2019), up to the maximum distance occupied across mice (580\\u2009cm); note that some bins during the teleport period will include data from fewer animals (Extended Data Fig. 5). To simulate the \\u2018ideal\\u2019 RR remapping destination if RR fields moved by the exact amount that the reward zone moved, we added the circular difference between reward zone starts to the pre-switch peak activity position of each RR cell.\\n132:We first fit five different time warp model types\\u2014shift, linear, piecewise one knot, piecewise two knots and piecewise three knots36\\u2014on the matrix of speed profiles within a trial set (i trials\\u2009\\u00d7\\u2009j linear position bins\\u2009\\u00d7\\u20091, expanded in the third dimension for compatibility with the time warp algorithm). These models apply warping functions of increasing nonlinear complexity to stretch and compress the data on each trial for maximal alignment. We included both rewarded and omission trials in the fitting procedure to find the best alignment across them. Model fit was assessed using the mean squared error between the time-warped speed profile of each trial and the mean across time-warped trials (Extended Data Fig. 8a). The piecewise three knots model most often produced the best fit (lowest mean squared error) across sessions (Extended Data Fig. 8c). We therefore re-fit all sessions with piecewise three knots to ensure that model selection could not influence the results. We then applied the model transform to the deconvolved neural activity matrix for the same set of trials (i trials\\u2009\\u00d7\\u2009j linear position bins\\u2009\\u00d7\\u2009n neurons; excluding neural data at movement speeds of <2\\u2009cm\\u2009s\\u22121). For neural analysis in Fig. 6, we then focused on trial sets before the switch in which the reward zone was at position \\u2018A\\u2019 or \\u2018B\\u2019, because the mean rewarded versus omission speed profiles were more similar on these trials than after the switch (Extended Data Fig. 8f) or when the reward was at \\u2018C\\u2019 (as there is less room at the end of the track for running speed to reach its maximum from position \\u2018C\\u2019 on omission trials; see Extended Data Figs. 1 and 8h for examples; see Extended Data Fig. 8 for analyses of trials after the switch and for all reward zones).\\n148:Our design matrix included three task variables and three movement variables (Extended Data Fig. 9a). Task variables were linear track position (from 0 to 450\\u2009cm), RR position (from \\u2212\\u03c0 to \\u03c0, centered around the start of each reward zone at 0\\u2009radians) and \\u2018rewarded\\u2019, a binary that switches from 0 to 1 when the reward is delivered on each trial and stays at 1 until the end of the trial (stays at 0 on omission trials). Linear track position and RR position were separately expanded into 45 cosine basis functions, one for each 10\\u2009cm or ~0.14 radian bin. \\u2018Rewarded\\u2019 was multiplied by the linear position basis functions to yield the interaction between position and whether the animal had been rewarded on a given trial.\\n152:All predictors were concatenated into a design matrix with 156 total features: 45 position bases\\u2009+\\u200945 RR position bases\\u2009+\\u200945 rewarded-by-position bases\\u2009+\\u20097 speed bases\\u2009+\\u20097 acceleration bases\\u2009+\\u20097 licking bases. The features were independently z-scored across samples before model fitting on the \\u2018response matrix\\u2019 of deconvolved activity of all cells (samples\\u2009\\u00d7\\u2009neurons).\\n156:Trial identities were used to group data for training and testing. Individual sessions (including trials both before and after the reward switch) were split into 85% training trials and 15% testing trials, using the GroupShuffleSplit method of Python\\u2019s sklearn package with a consistent random seed. Specifically, trials were allocated such that the training trials included 85% of rewarded trials before the switch, 85% of rewarded trials after the switch and 85% of all omission trials. The training trials were further divided for fivefold cross-validation during the fitting procedure. An optimal model was selected according to the deviance explained on the fivefold cross-validation data. We then tested this model on the held-out test data to assess model performance as the fraction deviance explained (FDE):\\n162:On average, the model\\u2019s FDE for all place cells was 0.10\\u2009\\u00b1\\u20090.19 (mean\\u2009\\u00b1\\u2009s.d.) (Extended Data Fig. 9b,c), 0.32\\u2009\\u00b1\\u20090.13 for TR cells, 0.29\\u2009\\u00b1\\u20090.11 for RR cells (examples in Fig. 7a, b) and 0.29\\u2009\\u00b1\\u20090.11 for non-RR remapping cells. For analysis of the relative contribution of individual variables, we only included cells with FDE\\u2009>\\u20090.15 in accordance with previous procedures37.\\n174:We applied previously developed factorized k-means and distance score algorithms to identify remapping times at the trial-by-trial level. Here, we used the deconvolved activity matrices of each neuron (i trials\\u2009\\u00d7\\u2009j 10\\u2009cm linear position bins), smoothed with a 10\\u2009cm\\u2009s.d. Gaussian. We first scaled activity between 0 and 1 to be comparable across neurons, normalizing each neuron to itself by subtracting its minimum and dividing by the difference between maximum and minimum activity over the session. We then created a population vector for each subpopulation of interest (i trials\\u2009\\u00d7\\u2009j position bins\\u2009\\u00d7\\u2009n neurons), used to create the population correlation matrices shown in Fig. 8 and Extended Data Fig. 10 (see \\u2018Trial-by-trial similarity matrices\\u2019). To identify two discrete clusters in the population activity corresponding to a pre-reward-switch and post-switch \\u2018map\\u2019, we fit a factorized k-means model with k\\u2009=\\u20092 and 100 restarts using the open source lvl package38. This model factorizes the normalized population activity array Xijn as:\\n180:To determine whether the k\\u2009=\\u20092 model fit was significantly better than could be expected from a session with only one \\u2018map\\u2019, we first cross-validated the model performance using a randomized speckled holdout of 10% of the data over 50 repetitions, then compared the uncentered test R2 to the test performance on a shuffled dataset. The shuffle was generated by random rotations of the population vector across trials38. Sessions in which the real model\\u2019s performance at k\\u2009=\\u20092 clusters exceeded that of the shuffle (two-sided Wilcoxon signed-rank test, P\\u2009<\\u20090.05) were accepted for further analysis (n\\u2009=\\u200950 out of 77 sessions for the RR population vector). We also confirmed that results were similar when restricting analysis to sessions best fit by k\\u2009=\\u20092 clusters compared to k\\u2009=\\u20093 or k\\u2009=\\u20094 using a silhouette score approach.\\n182:Next, we standardized the cluster labels such that cluster one was always the first cluster appearing in the session (that is, before the reward switch, though we did not restrict the number of trials that could be assigned to cluster one), and cluster two was always the second. We then computed a distance score of how close the population vector activity is to each cluster centroid on each trial38, for which a distance score of \\u22121 corresponds to the first cluster in the session and +1 corresponds to the second cluster. For single cells, the trial-by-trial correlation matrix C was computed as:\\n93:Using the spatially binned, deconvolved activity smoothed with a 10\\u2009cm\\u2009s.d. Gaussian, excluding activity from movement speeds <2\\u2009cm\\u2009s\\u22121, candidate place fields were identified as regions of \\u226520\\u2009cm that exceeded 20% of the maximum of the trial-averaged activity19 (over the 30 pre-switch trials or the final 30 post-switch trials). To include a given field, the cell was required to be significantly active within the field boundaries in at least eight (>25%) of the 30 trials, where \\u2018significantly active\\u2019 was defined as the raw, un-binned deconvolved activity at those positions exceeding its mean +1\\u2009s.d. across the 30 trials. For analyses of place field properties before versus after a reward switch, cells were excluded that did not have significant SI or a significant field both before and after the switch. The fraction of cell remapping types meeting these criteria on specific days is reported in Extended Data Fig. 3c. For analyses of place field width and in-field activity rate, cells were further excluded if one of their fields overlapped the first or last spatial bin of the track, as this prevented accurate detection of field boundaries that extended into the teleport zone (which was not imaged for some animals; see \\u20182P imaging\\u2019). Place field width was defined as the last minus the first position bin that crossed the initial 20% activity threshold.\\n97:We assessed the alignment of the center of mass of each field to RR or TR coordinates. We first confirmed that for cells with one field before and after the switch, the field center of mass replicated the robust RR and TR remapping patterns (Extended Data Fig. 3e) observed for spatial firing peaks (Fig. 2c,d,f). Next, for the subset of cells that had exactly two fields both before and after the switch, we computed the circular\\u2013circular correlation of the offsets between fields before versus after the switch (Extended Data Fig. 3f,g).\\n104:We implemented a previously developed decoder that uses circular\\u2013linear regression38 to predict the animal\\u2019s position in circular, RR coordinates from the deconvolved calcium event timeseries (at speeds of >2\\u2009cm\\u2009s\\u22121) of RR, TR or non-RR remapping neurons. The model \\u2018decode score\\u2019 at each timepoint t is given by \\\\cos ({{y}_{t}}-\\\\hat{{y}}_{t}), where is the animal\\u2019s true RR position and \\\\hat{{y}}_{t} is the predicted RR position. A score of one indicates perfect prediction and zero indicates random prediction. We performed tenfold cross-validation in which we trained the decoder on 90% of the data and tested it on a held-out 10%, either training and testing within the trials before the switch or training on one set (before) and testing on the other set (after). Figure 3a shows decoder performance from a single cross-validation fold, considering all data from an example session. To quantify decoding accuracy compared to chance, we next downsampled the data to match occupancy at each RR position bin (bin size 2\\u03c0\\u2009/\\u200945, ~10\\u2009cm), either within trials before the switch or in the last 30 trials after the switch (thereby matching trial numbers before and after). We then repeated model training and testing with 100 shuffled datasets for each session and cell subpopulation. In each shuffle, the timeseries of each cell was independently circularly shifted by a uniform random amount up to the number of samples in the session minus one. In Fig. 3b, model performance is reported as a mean decode score over time samples and cross-validation folds compared to the mean of the shuffles for each session. To evaluate decoder performance across sessions, we z-scored the decoder score of each session to its shuffle and binned the z-score as a function of RR position, then averaged across animals (Fig. 3c).\\n152:All predictors were concatenated into a design matrix with 156 total features: 45 position bases\\u2009+\\u200945 RR position bases\\u2009+\\u200945 rewarded-by-position bases\\u2009+\\u20097 speed bases\\u2009+\\u20097 acceleration bases\\u2009+\\u20097 licking bases. The features were independently z-scored across samples before model fitting on the \\u2018response matrix\\u2019 of deconvolved activity of all cells (samples\\u2009\\u00d7\\u2009neurons).\\n\"\n  2185→          }\n  2186→        ]\n  2187→      },\n  2188→      \"extra\": {\n  2189→        \"raw_arguments\": \"{\\\"cmd\\\":\\\"rg -n \\\\\\\"Fig. 3|decoder performance|decode score|z-scored|z-scored decoder|reward-relative position|decoded\\\\\\\" methods.txt\\\",\\\"workdir\\\":\\\"/app\\\",\\\"yield_time_ms\\\":1000,\\\"max_output_tokens\\\":4000}\"\n  2190→      }\n  2191→    },\n  2192→    {\n  2193→      \"step_id\": 88,\n  2194→      \"timestamp\": \"2026-03-11T20:47:24.146Z\",\n  2195→      \"source\": \"agent\",\n  2196→      \"model_name\": \"gpt-5.4\",\n  2197→      \"message\": \"The local `methods.txt` gives the processing details, but not the numeric Fig. 3 accuracy values. Then adds timeseries:\n```python\nsess.add_timeseries(licks=sess.vr_data['lick'],\n                    rewards=sess.vr_data['reward'],\n                    speed=sess.vr_data['speed'])\nsess.add_pos_binned_trial_matrix(['licks', 'rewards'], 'pos', impute_nans=False)\n```\n\n**`load_sess_pickle`** / **`get_sess_pkl_path`** (utilities.py): Loads previously saved session pickle files from `<preprocessed_root>/sess/<animal>/<date>/<scene>_<session>_<scan>.pickle`.\n\n**`multi_anim_sess`** (utilities.py lines 707+): The main entry point for loading data across animals for a given experiment day. For each animal: loads the sess pickle, computes dF/F and optionally deconvolved events, gets trial types/reward zones/trial subsets, and computes place cells.\n\n### How Trials are Defined/Segmented\n\nThe session object stores two key arrays:\n- `sess.trial_start_inds` -- 1 x N_trials array of sample indices for trial starts\n- `sess.teleport_inds` -- 1 x N_trials array of sample indices for trial ends (teleport)\n\n**`get_trial_types`** (behavior.py lines 37-76): Iterates over trials using `sess.trial_start_inds[trial]` to `sess.teleport_inds[trial]`. For each trial, checks `sess.vr_data['reward']` for any reward > 0 and `sess.vr_data['rzone']` for any rzone > 0. A trial is rewarded only if both reward delivery AND reward zone occupancy occurred:\n```python\nisreward = np.append(isreward, (np.any(tmp_reward > 0) and np.any(tmp_rzone > 0)) * 1)\n```\n\n**`get_reward_zones`** (behavior.py lines 77-158): Maps scene names to reward zone coordinates using a dictionary:\n```python\nreward_zone_dict = {'A': [175, 225], 'B': [390, 440], '1': [325, 375], ...\n                    'X': [80, 130], 'Y': [200, 250], 'Z': [320, 370], ...}\n```\nNote: Labels A/B/C map to coordinates X/Y/Z respectively. For switch sessions, set0 = chronologically first zone, set1 = second. For single-zone sessions, splits into halves if `force_two_sets=True`.\n\n### How Neural Data is Processed (dF/F, Deconvolution, Filtering)\n\n**`dff`** (preprocessing.py lines 289+): Core dF/F computation function. Key parameters:\n- `f`: ROI fluorescence (cells x samples)\n- `trial_starts`, `teleports`: trial boundary indices\n- `f_neu`: neuropil fluorescence\n- `neuropil_method`: typically `'subtract'` with `neu_coef=0.7`\n- `baseline_method`: `'maximin'` (20s sliding window, computed per trial)\n- `deconvolve`: optional, uses `suite2p.extraction.dcnv` (OASIS algorithm)\n- `keep_teleports`: whether to include inter-trial periods\n\n**Critical trial masking logic** -- fluorescence is set to NaN outside of trial boundaries:\n```python\nf_ = np.zeros(f.shape) * np.nan\nfor i, (start, stop) in enumerate(zip(start_inds, stop_inds)):\n    f_[:, start - 1:stop - 1] = f[:, start - 1:stop - 1]\n```\n\nWhen `keep_teleports=False`: `start_inds = trial_starts.tolist()`, `stop_inds = teleports.tolist()`.\nWhen `keep_teleports=True`: `start_inds = np.append(trial_starts[0], np.array(teleports[:-1] + 2)).tolist()`.\n\nThe processing pipeline:\n1. Neuropil subtraction: `f_ -= neu_coef * f_neu_` (neu_coef = 0.7)\n2. Baseline calculation: maximin filter per trial with 20s window\n3. dF/F = (F - baseline) / |baseline|, smoothed with 2-sample Gaussian\n4. Deconvolution: OASIS algorithm via Suite2p, producing `events`\n\n**Interneuron exclusion**: Cells with Pearson correlation > 0.5 between dF/F and running speed are excluded.\n\n### How Lick Data is Processed and Filtered\n\nFrom `get_timeseries_data` (glmUtils.py):\n```python\nlicks = np.squeeze(np.copy(sess.timeseries['licks']))\n```\n\n**Lick sensor error detection** (per trial):\n```python\nif sum(licks[start:stop] > 2)/len(licks[start-1:stop-1]) > 0.35:\n    licks[start:stop] = np.nan\n```\nIf >35% of samples in a trial have cumulative lick count > 2, the entire trial's licks are set to NaN (erroneous sensor).\n\n**Lick binarization**:\n```python\nlicks[licks > 1] = 1\nlicks[findnans] = np.nan\n```\nLicks stored as cumulative counts per imaging frame are clipped to binary (0 or 1).\n\nFrom `methods.txt`: ~0.65% of all trials (81 out of 12,376) were removed. Detection threshold: >30% of 0.0645s imaging frame samples with cumulative lick count > 2 (producing sustained lick rate >= 20 Hz). Note: the code uses 0.35 (35%) as threshold while the paper says 30% -- a minor discrepancy.\n\n**For the GLM/decoder**, licks are additionally smoothed with a Gaussian:\n```python\ndata['licks'] = ut.nansmooth(licks[mask_out_nans], lick_smooth_sigma)  # sigma=2\n```\n\n### How Reward Outcomes are Determined\n\n**`get_reward_inds`** (rewardAnalysis.py): `np.where(sess.vr_data['reward']>0)[0]`\n\n**`get_trial_types`** (behavior.py): A trial is \"rewarded\" if `reward > 0` AND `rzone > 0` occurred within the trial window.\n\n**`get_omission_trials`** (rewardAnalysis.py): Finds unrewarded trials where the reward zone was never active (true omissions vs. \"lapsed\" trials):\n```python\nfor i,(t_start,t_end) in enumerate(zip(t_starts,t_ends)):\n    if not np.any(sess.vr_data.iloc[t_start:t_end]['rzone']>0):\n        # true omission -- no reward zone active\n```\n\n**In `get_timeseries_data`**: `was_reward` is a state variable that switches from 0 to 1 at reward delivery and stays 1 until trial end. `was_omission` switches from 0 to 1 at reward-zone entry on omission trials.\n\n### How Speed is Handled\n\nSpeed comes from `sess.vr_data['speed']` (cm/s) and is stored in `sess.timeseries['speed']` as a smoothed running speed per imaging frame.\n\n**In `get_timeseries_data`**:\n```python\ntmp_speed = np.squeeze(np.copy(sess.timeseries['speed'][0]))\ntmp_speed[findnans] = np.nan\nif use_speed_thr is not None:\n    tmp_speed[tmp_speed < use_speed_thr] = np.nan  # default use_speed_thr=2\n```\nSamples below the speed threshold (default 2 cm/s) are set to NaN and then excluded from the final mask: `mask_out_nans = ((~np.isnan(tmp_speed)) & (~np.isnan(licks)))`.\n\nFrom `methods.txt`: \"excluding activity from movement speeds < 2 cm/s\" is used for place field identification, decoder analysis, and GLM.\n\n### What `get_timeseries_data` Does\n\nLocated in `glmUtils.py` (lines 33-200+). This is the function that prepares continuous time-series data for the decoder and GLM analyses. It:\n\n1. Copies position from `sess.vr_data['pos']`\n2. Initializes NaN arrays for: `deconv_activity`, `rel_pos`, `pos`, `trial_ids`, `was_reward`, `was_omission`\n3. Copies licks from `sess.timeseries['licks']`\n4. Gets omission indices and reward zone coordinates\n5. Loops over each trial (start, stop from `trial_starts`, `teleports`):\n   - Computes reward-relative position (circular, using `spatial.pos_cm_to_rad`)\n   - Copies position and deconvolved activity for the trial window `[start-1:stop-1]`\n   - Assigns trial ID to all samples in the trial\n   - Sets `was_reward` as a state variable (0 before reward delivery, 1 after)\n   - Sets `was_omission` similarly for omission trials\n   - Detects lick sensor errors (>35% of samples with lick count > 2 -> NaN)\n6. Post-loop cleanup:\n   - Binarizes licks (>1 -> 1)\n   - Applies speed threshold (< 2 cm/s -> NaN)\n   - Creates final mask excluding NaN speed and NaN licks\n7. Returns a DataFrame with columns: `rel_pos`, `pos`, `trials`, `speed`, `accel`, `licks`, `rewarded`, `omission` (all masked) and the masked deconvolved activity matrix.\n\n### The trial_start/teleport Indexing (the -1 Offset)\n\nThis is a critical detail found throughout the reference code.
